| **73** | **A `MODIFY` FRAGMENT RUN *WITHOUT* `--write` CHANGED THE MODEL FOR REAL AND NOTHING COULD UNDO IT.** `--write` wraps a run in a `TransactionGroup` that is rolled back unless `apply` is given - that is the whole safety story, and it only covers changes Revit makes INSIDE a transaction. **Unloading a link is not one.** Run without `--write` on `Snowdon-scratch`, `unload-links` reported `unloaded 6` and **all six links in the owner's open model were genuinely unloaded**: `list-linked-models` then said `notLoaded 6`. There was no transaction, so there was nothing to roll back and no undo step. They were reloaded immediately - `notLoaded 0`, `RVT Links` back to 6, model still 9,638 elements - but nothing in the tooling made that happen or would have noticed if it had not. **THE SAFETY MODEL READS AS "NO `--write` MEANS NO CHANGE" AND THAT IS NOT WHAT IT GUARANTEES.** It guarantees that TRANSACTIONAL work is rolled back. Document-level operations - link loading, and anything else Revit does outside a transaction - pass straight through. `unload-links` and `reload-links` are `risk: MODIFY`, so the risk gate was satisfied and the transaction gate simply did not apply. **AND IT ANSWERED A QUESTION THE FILE ASKS ABOUT ITSELF.** `unload-links`' header says *"whether Revit permits it inside an open transaction is UNPROVEN here... guessing either way would harden into a fact nobody checked"*. Now measured, both ways: **inside** a transaction (`--write`) -> `unloaded 0, refused 6`, Revit refuses every one; **outside** -> `unloaded 6, refused 0`. So the fragment can only work on the path that cannot be rolled back, which is worth knowing before anybody runs it on a job model. | **OPEN and it is the owner's call.** The proofs are real and both are ready to sign; the hazard is that proving them required changing his model with no undo |

The two surviving elements were NOT identified, so "they are unrelated host elements" follows from the ids belonging to another document rather than from inspecting them. **The safe behaviour and the dangerous one differ only by whether an id number happens to be in use twice.** | **OPEN - THE LOSS IS VISIBLE NOW AND THE WRONG BINDING IS NOT FIXED, 2026-09-20.** The state still says OPEN because the repair this row names - a carried value that says which DOCUMENT it came from - is not built, and two unrelated host elements are still bound. **WHAT CHANGED IS THAT IT NO LONGER READS LIKE SUCCESS.** `RevitFragment.Size` takes the carried value beside the shaped one and reports **`(2 of 1128)`** where it used to report `(2)`, which was indistinguishable from a deliberate narrowing to two. **THE MECHANISM WAS CONFIRMED FROM THE SOURCE RATHER THAN RE-RUN**, which this container can do and a Revit is not needed for: `Shape()` walks each carried `ElementId` through `doc.GetElement(id)` on the HOST document, swallows the miss (`try { ... } catch { }`), keeps only what resolved, and returns `live.Count == 0 ? null : live`. **The zero case was thought about and the partial case was not** - the comment on that very line reads *"Held ids, resolved none - not the same as having been empty"*. **NO THRESHOLD, DELIBERATELY**: *refuse below some percentage* needs a floor, and R-60 says a floor is derived from a measurement or it is not set at all. A plain write leaves a **truncated** file if interrupted; delete-then-move leaves **no file at all**, through a window made wider by the extra syscall inside it. **In `HeronConfig` it does not corrupt the settings, it erases them, silently:** `Load` answers a missing file with `Defaults` and swallows `IOException` without a word, and the next `Save` writes those defaults back - so the file whose own header says *"Owned by you. Never overwritten by an update"* is gone with no message. In `BridgeIdentity` the comment read *"so a client never reads a half-written file"*, which is true and does not cover a client reading **no** file and concluding no bridge is running. Until one is open this row cannot be closed or dismissed, and no claim is made here that it is slow, because nothing has yet timed it at a size where it could be - and `check-minimum-clearance` **already reports `pairsChecked`**, so one run on a real model answers it without new tooling. **The fix is not a swap.** A spatial filter finds what intersects; clearance is about what does NOT touch yet, so the outline has to be grown by the threshold first. Substring(0, 60) + "..."`. Both behaviours are right on their own: a list preview exists so a proof can see WHAT came back without printing forty rows. **THE MISTAKE IS DECLARING A ONE-OF-ONE AS A LIST.** The fragment's checks are a single if/else chain, so at most ONE reason is ever produced - the list described the fragment wrongly before it truncated anything. Changed to `string`, and Revit's two refusals then arrived in full and in one line. **THE RULE WORTH CARRYING: where the MESSAGE is the answer rather than a sample of many, declare it a string.** A count is honest about a list; its contents are not. | **116** | **THE FOURTEEN CROSSINGS WERE NEVER A RANKING PROBLEM - THEY NEVER REACHED THE RANKING'S SAFETY NET AT ALL.** [Row 113](#) recorded 14 questions answered by a write and proposed no fix, on the grounds that neither retrieval route was at fault. Both observations were right and the conclusion was wrong. `heron_retrieve.find` tries `SEARCH.short_circuit` FIRST: an exactly declared sentence resolves by IDENTITY and no ranking runs. **TWO REASONS A SENTENCE MISSES THAT ROUTE, AND THE LIBRARY HAD BOTH.** (1) **AMBIGUITY.** `identities` stores `__ambiguous__` when two fragments declare one phrase, and that is a deliberate MISS - so the request falls through to ranking. Only **7 of 2,442** phrases were ambiguous, and two were dangerous: *"can I edit these"* was claimed by `read-element-ownership` AND `report-element-ownership`, so it fell through and was answered by **`UPDATE_SAVED_SET`** - and `report-element-ownership`'s own identity is literally *"whether these elements can be edited"*. *"zoom to these"* was claimed by `zoom-to-elements` and by **`set-view-section-box`**, a MODIFY. (2) **NOBODY DECLARED IT.** The other 12 are sentences no fragment claims, so identity cannot fire and ranking decides - which is the same fact [row 113](#) found from the other end. **THE FIX IS DECLARING, NOT DEMOTING**, and a nudge could never have done it: `heron_retrieve`'s quality nudge is deliberately smaller than ONE fusion rank (0.00026) while these writes won by **2.4 to 2.6 ranks**. Resolving the two ambiguities and claiming six sentences for `COUNT_ELEMENTS`, `MEASURE_MEP_SLOPE`, `DESCRIBE_BLANK_PARAMETERS` and `REPORT_SHEET_TITLE_BLOCKS` took the measured count from **14 to 7**, and the four worst are gone: *"how many pipes are there"* no longer caps open pipe ends, *"count the air terminals"* no longer connects them, *"which parameters are empty"* no longer removes values, and *"what is the slope of this pipe"* no longer re-slopes it. **THE TOOL WAS ALSO OVER-REPORTING.** It flagged anything that was not `READ`; docs/12 s2 gives `ANALYZE` side effects **none**. Corrected to `MODIFY`/`PUBLISH`/`ADMIN`, with `EXECUTE` counted separately as a VIEW change - which immediately named `DESCRIBE_BLANK_PARAMETERS` as the right owner it had previously hidden. | **OPEN - 7 of 14 fixed, and THE SWEEP THAT MEASURES IT IS NOT DETERMINISTIC, found 2026-09-17 while trying to reproduce this very number. THIS IS THE LIVE ROW for the whole thread; [109](#) and [113](#) were folded into it the same day.** Three runs of `check-risk-crossings.py`, same code, same `%APPDATA%` store, same machine, minutes apart, returned **7, then 9, then 8** crossings - and **the second and third had byte-identical inputs**. So a count from this tool is a sample, not a measurement, and *"measured both times"* above is two samples that happened to agree. **The seven this row names are the stable part**: they appear in all three runs, by name. **What moves is `how many pipes are there` -> `CAP_OPEN_PIPE_ENDS`, which this row lists among the four worst as FIXED** - it came back in one run of three. An intermittent crossing is worse than a steady one, because the fix looks like it held. **DIAGNOSED the same day, and it is not the ranker.** Ruled out by measurement rather than by reading: *the ranking* - one process answered 5/5 identically and three separate processes 3/3, and every sort ends `(-score, keyword_rank, c.id)` so a tie cannot break two ways; *the cache* - `utterances` holds **0** rows, so `recall()` cannot fire, and `remember()` refuses anything but `RAN` evidence exactly so it cannot; *a swallowed lock* - every `sqlite3.OperationalError` handler in the read path narrows to `"no such table"` and re-raises (D-52 holding), and no sweep printed a `NOT RESOLVED`; *a half-read rebuild* - `heron_search.index()` commits **once**, so a reader sees the old index or the new one, never a partial; *a different fragment tree* - all five worktrees on this PC declare the sentence. **WHAT IT IS: the sentence that moves is the only one of the three whose right answer never goes through ranking at all.** `how many pipes are there` -> `FRG-ELE-002` = `COUNT_ELEMENTS` is a DECLARED IDENTITY - which is precisely how this row fixed it - so it is answered by `short_circuit` in one lookup. When the sweep saw `CAP_OPEN_PIPE_ENDS`, **the identity route MISSED and the ranked search answered instead**, and ranking is the thing that answers a question with a write. So the instability lives in the INDEX, not the ranker - which is why the seven crossings above, all sentences nobody declares, are rock-steady, and the one this row calls FIXED is the one that wobbles. **A fix that works by declaring a phrase is only as stable as the table that holds it, and `index()` DELETEs `identities` and `fragment_text` wholesale and rebuilds them.** **AND THE SWEEP IS NOT READ-ONLY, WHICH ITS DOCSTRING CLAIMS IT IS**: run against a private copy of the store that nothing else on the machine can reach, `check-risk-crossings.py` changed that copy's md5 - **`63e333e9a28e...` before, `9d132778d8f7...` after, one sweep, no other writer**. So a sweep perturbs the index it is measuring. **That controlled run also returned the STABLE eight** (the seven, plus row 109's sentence), which is the fourth run in the set: 7, 9, 8 against the shared store and 8 against a copy only this session could touch - **the outlier is the one that shared the store with somebody**. The shared store ALSO moved while this session was pointed at the copy, so another session writes it too - `%APPDATA%\Heron\knowledge\global.db` is one file for every worktree. **NOT ESTABLISHED, and it is the remaining question:** which write made the identity miss on that one run. Comparing the shared store against the copy showed **only FTS5 shadow tables** differing - `fragments` 372, `identities` 2448, `vectors` 374, `capabilities` 373, `utterances` 0, all equal - so the write that was caught in the act was structural and does not by itself explain a miss. **The four runs are kept** in [work-notes/investigations](work-notes/investigations/2026-09-17-risk-crossings-not-deterministic.md), because a conclusion is only re-checkable if the output it came from is. **The cheap next step is in the tool, not the ranker:** make the sweep print a fingerprint of the index it ran against - the `identities` count and a store hash - so that two runs can be compared at all. Until then, read the NAMES and not the number. The remaining seven need an owner chosen per sentence and none was obvious enough to claim at speed: duct size, pipe diameter, an element's workset, what is missing its Mark, views on a sheet, insulation thickness, and selecting pipes in a view. **Five ambiguous phrases also remain**, all read-vs-read so none is dangerous, each needing a judgement about which fragment owns the sentence. **THAT SENTENCE WAS WRONG WHEN IT WAS WRITTEN AND IS TRUE NOW BY REPAIR, 2026-09-20.** It was **six**, and two were MODIFY against MODIFY - [row 134](#) measured it two ways the day after this was written. | **130** | **`heron_lookup` TOLD A MODELLER WITH TWO REVITS OPEN THAT NO REVIT WAS CONNECTED, AND SKIPPED THE VERSION FILTER WHILE SAYING SO.** Measured 2026-09-19 with sessions 20472 and 36908 both live. `heron_lookup("how many ducts are there")` answered correctly on the routing - `FILTER_ELEMENTS_BY_CATEGORY`, matched by **identity**, provided by a PROVEN fragment - and then printed: **"No Revit is connected, so the version filter did not run."** Two were. `revit_use_session 36908` in the very next call returned *"Now working with Revit 2024 (session 36908) - test projject, 3,594 elements"*, and the identical lookup then printed **"Filtered to Revit 2024 (chosen)."** **THE CAUSE IS A FALL-THROUGH, AND THE FUNCTION'S OWN DOCSTRING DESCRIBES ONLY ONE OF ITS TWO EXITS.** `_revit_version()` in `heron_mcp_server.py` returns the bound session's release when one is chosen, ASSUMES the release when exactly one Revit is live - and naming that assumption is careful, deliberate work - and then `return None, None`. That last line is reached when **two or more are connected and none has been chosen**, which is a completely different fact from nothing being connected. The docstring says *"Returns (None, None) when nothing is connected"*, which is true of one path and not of the other. **THE WORDING IS THE SMALLER HALF.** The version filter genuinely did not run, so on that call no fragment was checked against the release it would run on - and the configuration where that check matters MOST is precisely two Revits open, because they can be different releases. A 2025-only fragment could be offered to somebody working in 2020, with a line above it saying the filter was skipped for a reason that is not true. **IT IS THE SAME FAMILY AS [ROW 117](#) AND [PR #188](https://github.com/Ajmalpshaik/Heron-AI/pull/188)**: a message describing one state while the system is in another, and both of those also surfaced only with two Revits open. | **FIXED 2026-09-20, AND IT WAS THE TWO SENTENCES THIS CELL ASKED FOR.** `heron_session.version_for_filter(live, chosen_pid, chosen_explicitly)` decides the release and says HOW, and **`how` is never `None`** - an answer with no release still names why, because *nothing is connected* and *two are and you have not picked* send a reader to different actions (D-52). **Six answers where the old body had four, and one of those four covered three different situations** - `(None, None)` was returned alike for *nothing is connected*, for *several connected and none chosen*, and for [row 159](#)'s *the Revit you chose has closed*. The new one that matters here is **`N Revits are connected and none has been chosen`** - with the count in it, because a reader told *2 are connected* knows there is something to pick between where *none is connected* sends them to start Revit. Both call sites print that sentence instead of the one that was wrong, and the several-connected case adds **what to do**: *Pick one with `revit_use_session <pid>` and ask again - with more than one release open, the filter is the thing that matters most.* **THE RULE IS PURE AND LIVES IN `mcp/server/heron_session.py`; ONLY THE PLUMBING STAYED IN `_revit_version`** - gather, ask, close. That half stays reasoned from six outcomes the pure rule returns, and the distinction between the two is the point of saying which is which. **AND READING THE SAME EIGHT LINES FOUND A SECOND DEFECT THIS ROW DOES NOT NAME**, recorded apart as [row 159](#): with the CHOSEN session closed and one other live, the old body fell through and returned the survivor's release as *the only Revit connected* - the wall sliding onto a release nobody picked, against this file's own table. **WHAT THIS ROW SAID WHILE IT WAS OPEN, kept because the next step it named is the one that was taken:** Found 2026-09-19 while running skills end to end the way a modeller would. The repair is two sentences rather than one - `_revit_version` should distinguish *nothing connected* from *several connected, none chosen*, and the caller should say which. Python only, so no Revit restart; the Claude app must be restarted for `mcp/` to reload |
| **131** | **THE KNOWLEDGE STORE IS SHARED BY EVERY SESSION AND IS REBUILT FROM WHICHEVER WORKTREE INDEXED LAST, SO ONE SESSION'S FRAGMENT EDIT SILENTLY DISAPPEARS FROM ANOTHER'S.** Measured 2026-09-19. Two utterances were added to `count-elements/fragment.yaml` to stop *"how many sprinklers on level 2"* resolving to `PLACE_FAMILY_INSTANCES`. After rebuilding, both resolved by identity to `FRG-ELE-002`, confirmed in the `identities` table and live through `heron_lookup`. Its own docstring even names the split it does not cross: *"docs/06 section 2 lists Backup in the Data class"*. It was right about its scope and nothing owned the other one | **FIXED AND PROVED 2026-09-19, ON ALL THREE RELEASES INSTALLED ON THE OWNER'S PC, AND WRITTEN UP IN [docs/07 section 10](07-installation-and-update.md).** Built into `tools/deploy-addin.ps1`: every deploy now copies the install it is about to replace into `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Heron\install-backup\<release>\` with a `replaced.json` recording the release, the UTC time, the assembly version, its SHA-256 and the file count; `-Rollback` puts it back and **verifies what was WRITTEN rather than what was intended**, the same discipline the deploy path already used; `-Remove` backs up first, because an uninstall is what somebody reaches for when things are already going wrong. The copy is taken at the LAST moment before overwriting, after every other check has passed, so a run that throws on the wrong build flavour does not spend the rollback point on a no-op. **THE PROOF IS A ROUND TRIP WITH A GENUINELY DIFFERENT BUILD, NOT A FILE COPY CHECKED BY EYE.** For each release a second build was made with `-p:Version=0.1.1`, deployed OVER the good one to stand in for an update that turns out badly, then rolled back, then every file in the folder hashed and compared: **2020** `2B3FA216` -> `C119604C` -> `2B3FA216`, **22 files restored, 0 differing**; **2024** `8A3922C3` -> `6E712353` -> `8A3922C3`, **22 restored, 0 differing**; **2027** `5B5935C8` -> `D34B37DF` -> `5B5935C8`, **15 restored, 0 differing**. Manifest restored all three times, `heron.config` unchanged throughout at `9FC06D52`. **The counts differ on purpose** - 22 against 15 because the .NET Framework releases carry `System.*` compatibility assemblies that .NET 10 has in the box, and 2027 carries a `deps.json` they do not; a rollback restoring the same number everywhere would mean it was not reading what was there. **WHAT IT DOES NOT PROVE, SAID PLAINLY.** It proves the FILES come back. The restored install is byte-identical to the one proved to load in docs/07 section 10.3, so it loads for the same reason that one did - **that is inference, not an independent observation**, and no release was restarted a second time to make it one. **TWO LIMITS BUILT IN DELIBERATELY:** the copy is ONE deep, because an update that has gone wrong twice running is a case for rebuilding from source rather than for a longer history; and it lives in `%LOCALAPPDATA%`, which is machine-local and not roamed, so **losing the profile or the disk loses it**. Calling it a backup of the product would overstate it - the product's real home is git. **AND `-Remove` GAINED A GUARD IT NEVER HAD:** the running-Revit check now covers remove and rollback as well as install, because `Remove-Item` on an assembly Revit has loaded fails part-way through the folder, leaving a partial install and reading as *"the uninstall went wrong"* rather than *"close Revit first"*. **Row 147. The brief said take 141, and this row has now been renumbered TWICE** - PR #199 took 140-142 while this branch was open, then PR #201 took 143-146 while its CI was running. The instruction was correct when written, when 139 was the highest. **The lesson is not that the brief was wrong, it is that a row number cannot be reserved by intending to use it**: several sessions append to this file at once, and the only number that holds is one derived from the file at the moment of writing and committed straight away. Both renumbers moved the three citations with them - docs/07 section 10.6, NEEDS-CHECKING A13, and the message check-package.py prints - because a row that moves and leaves its references behind is worse than one never written |

| **157** | **THE RETRIEVER HAS BEEN SAYING "NO ROUTE PREFERRED THIS" IN PLAIN WORDS AT THE SEAM EVERY SWEEP READS, AND EVERY SWEEP THREW THE SENTENCE AWAY.** Found 2026-09-19 while looking for a way to see past [row 137](#)'s blind spot. That row's finding is that a question inside a **MODIFY** skill reaching an unrelated MODIFY is invisible **by construction**: a crossing is decided by comparing a sentence's reach against the SKILL's declared risk, so a skill declared MODIFY can never register one. Measured at the seam: *"what colour is the sky"* resolves to **`COLOR_BY_PARAMETER`, a MODIFY**, and the note says the words route *"matched 392 fragment(s) - at least as many as the 392 the filter left - so it ranked the library rather than selecting from it"*. **Heron said it had nothing, and `check-skill-routing.py`, `prove-skill.py` and the catalogue all recorded `(phrase, capability, risk, route)` and dropped the sentence** | **FIXED 2026-09-19, by READING what was already there rather than deriving anything.** `ROUTING.unsettled(note)` matches those four phrases and returns them as short tags; an empty result is **the absence of a complaint and not a clean answer** (D-52), and a note that could not be read is empty for the same reason. **NOTHING IS RE-DERIVED, AND THAT IS THE DESIGN.** A second implementation of *was this contested* would be a second opinion about the retriever's own measurement, which [row 154](#) is the cost of. **THE PHRASES ARE PINNED AGAINST `Contest.sentence`'s SOURCE** in `tests/test_skill_proving.py`, so a reword there fails loudly and names the phrase rather than quietly turning the section off - and the pin closes Python's adjacent-string-literal seams first, because a sentence a reader sees whole is broken across lines in the source and a literal search would report a reword that never happened. **IT CUTS ACROSS THE FIVE LISTS RATHER THAN BECOMING A SIXTH**, which is the whole point: an unsettled answer can be a reach, a miss, a crossing or an escalation, and a sixth bucket would make a reader choose. `check-skill-routing.py` prints it as its own section and says in that section why it exists; `prove-skill.py` records it as a **sixth field** and names it under every verdict. **A RECORDING WRITTEN BEFORE THIS STILL READS**: every consumer pads and slices rather than unpacking a fixed width, which `tests/test_skill_catalog.py` already relied on and now matters. **IT DOES NOT CHANGE A VERDICT WORD.** The retriever saying it found nothing is a question for a person, not a failure - the rule every report in `tools/` is written to - and a tool that downgraded a skill on it would be ranking confidence, which is R-56 and R-58's floor problem with a new name - both are blocked on W-8 for exactly that reason, as `tests/test_contest.py` and `tests/test_conflict.py` both record: *retrieval has no floor*, and R-60 says a floor is derived from a measurement or it is not set at all. **MEASURED ACROSS ALL FORTY-THREE SENTENCES, 2026-09-19**, by re-taking the routing recording (`routing-2026-09-19b.json`, twenty-five minutes, no Revit): **24 of 43 carry a complaint**, 21 of them *the words route ranked the library rather than selecting from it* and 9 *a coin toss*, some both. **The number that matters is where they landed**: 24 sentences reach a capability their skill declares, and **12 of those 24 carry a complaint** - half of the page's good news. **BOTH CROSSINGS CARRY ONE TOO.** The starkest is Heron's own opening example: *"select all the ducts"* reaches `SELECT_BY_CATEGORIES`, which `select-elements` declares, **by `hybrid` with the words route having ranked the library rather than selecting from it** - the right answer from a route with no claim on the sentence. *"select the 300x300 dampers"* carries **both** complaints. *"show me the ones you found"*, the one that resolves by **identity**, carries none - which is the argument for declaring a sentence rather than ranking for it, stated in data. **THE TWO COMPLAINTS ARE NOT THE SAME STRENGTH AND THE PAGE MUST NOT SAY THEY ARE**: *a coin toss* means NEITHER route preferred the winner; the words one means only that THAT route had no claim, and nearness may still have. So the card says **the retriever complained**, never that the answer is wrong - a first draft read *with no confidence* and was corrected before it landed. **AND THE SAME RE-TAKE SETTLED [ROW 152](#)'s DESIGN CHOICE WITH A MEASUREMENT.** The two recordings were taken against stores whose md5 **differs** (`c1193203...` against `e38e1298...`) and **not one of the 43 answers moved** - same capability, same risk, same route, every sentence. A staleness rule hung on the fingerprint would have called that recording stale and been wrong about all 43; the phrases, which had not moved either, called it current and were right. **AND THE SIXTH FIELD BROKE `--jobs`, WHICH NO TEST COVERED AND RUNNING IT FOUND IN ONE COMMAND.** `job_file()` unpacked FIVE values from a measured row, so `--jobs` **with** `--routing-from` died with *`ValueError: too many values to unpack (expected 5)`* while `--jobs --plan-only` was fine - every job case in `tests/test_skill_proving.py` passed `measured=None`, which is exactly why nothing caught it. **THREE FUNCTIONS UNPACK A MEASURED ROW AND ONLY TWO WERE PADDED**: `verdict`'s `said()` and the catalogue's `said()` were, `job_file` was not. (`load_routing` is not one of them - it passes each row through as a tuple and unpacks nothing, which is why it needed no change and why an earlier draft of this sentence naming it as padded was wrong; corrected 2026-09-20.) It now pads and slices like the others, and the suite emits a job file from a SIX-field measurement and from a FIVE-field one, asserting both parse. **The ten job files are re-emitted against the new recording**, so each now carries what the retriever said beside where each sentence landed - `check-connectivity`'s two crossings both do. **AND THE SUBTITLE COMPUTED THE HEADLINE NUMBER AND DROPPED IT, INSIDE THE CHANGE THAT ADDED THIS ROW.** The two caveats were written as `shaky + stale if stale else ""`, which Python reads as `(shaky + stale) if stale else ""` - so with no stale recording the COMPLAINT sentence vanished with it, and the page said *"1 have every word reaching"* while holding, unprinted, that **12 sentences across 7 skills reached and the retriever complained**. A precedence bug, and the exact failure this row is about, committed by the row's own author an hour after writing it. **Found by reading the RENDERED subtitle rather than the code** - which is the only way it could have been found, because the code looks right. The caveats are built as a plain variable now, and `tests/test_skill_catalog.py` pins the case that was missing: a complaint with **no** staleness beside it, which is the combination that vanished. **THIS ROW WAS ANNOUNCED AS 155 AND IS 157 ON PURPOSE, AND 156 IS RESERVED RATHER THAN MISSING** - two other branches had taken the numbers in between by the time this one was written. **155 is now on `main`** as [PR #203](https://github.com/Ajmalpshaik/Heron-AI/pull/203)'s *a test suite breaks 55 real `fragment.yaml` files*, merged while this branch was open; **[PR #204](https://github.com/Ajmalpshaik/Heron-AI/pull/204) announced 155 and 156 too** and will need one of its own moved when it rebases. So the later row moved - twice, honestly, each time off the `main` in front of it. That branch has already paid for the other resolution once: its own commit *"My renumbering made their row look missing, so I inserted a second copy of it"*. A gap is harmless and a duplicate is not, which is the rule [NEEDS-CHECKING](NEEDS-CHECKING.md) states - keep both, renumber the later one, check for duplicates afterwards. Verified: no duplicate number in section 5 |
| **158** | **TEN QUESTIONS ARE DECLARED, IN WRITING, ON FRAGMENTS THAT CHANGE THE MODEL - AND NEITHER ROUTING SWEEP CAN SEE ONE OF THEM.** Found 2026-09-19 while checking whether [row 146](#)'s four crossings had a READ to give them to. **THE CASE THAT STARTED IT:** *"what scale is this view"* resolves to `SET_VIEW_SCALE`, a **MODIFY**, by route **`identity`** - and `brain/fragments/set-view-scale/fragment.yaml` **line 127** declares that exact phrase in its own `utterances:` block. It is not a ranking accident and **no re-ranking repairs it**: identity short-circuits before ranking runs. **IT FALLS BETWEEN THE TWO SWEEPS BY CONSTRUCTION.** `check-routing.py` asks each fragment's own utterances back to the search and separates *a sentence a READ claims, answered by something that WRITES* - and it is blind here for **TWO INDEPENDENT REASONS, both read off its source rather than inferred**. Its first test keeps a row where `rung(risk[winner]) > rung(risk[claimer])`; a fragment declaring its OWN sentence and winning has winner == claimer, so the comparison is false and there is no crossing by construction. Its second opens `if risk_of(store, fid) != "READ": continue`, so a sentence claimed by a **MODIFY** is skipped on the first line - and two lines later it says `if served == fid: continue` with the belief written beside it: **`# the host answers correctly`**. That comment is the whole defect in four words. It is right about every case the tool was built for and wrong about this one, and nothing in the file is at fault - the question was never asked of it. `check-risk-crossings.py` asks sentences **nobody declares**, precisely because the first can only test what is declared - so a declared one is not in its list. **Checked rather than assumed: of its 78 questions, ZERO are among these ten.** And the near-miss is the whole story. Its closest sentence to *"what scale is this view"* is **"what is the scale of this view"** - five words shared, one phrasing apart - and [row 146](#) reports that one crossing to `SET_VIEW_SCALE` and calls it a **capability gap**, which it also is. **So two sweeps found two halves of one defect from opposite sides, a week apart, and neither could see the other half.** The hand-written phrasing crosses because nothing READ answers it; the declared phrasing crosses because the WRITE claims it in its own `utterances:` block. The first needs a new capability. The second needs one line deleted from a card. A reader given only row 146 would have built the fragment and left the declaration in place, and the sentence Ajmal actually says would have gone on landing on a write by identity, which no new READ can outrank. **It is [row 137](#)'s blind spot one layer down**, and like that one it needs no search at all to find: it is a property of the card. **ALL TEN, SWEPT OVER THE LIBRARY**: `SET_VIEW_SCALE` *"what scale is this view"*; `SET_VIEW_RANGE` *"what is the view range"*; `SET_PRINT_SETTINGS` *"what paper sizes can i print"*; `SET_DATUM_EXTENT_TYPE` *"is this grid 2d or 3d here"*; `ASSIGN_LOCATION_DATA` *"which room is each device in"*; `EXTRACT_CAD_CURVES` *"what layers are in this cad file"* and *"...in this dwg"*; `DIMENSION_FAMILY_INSTANCES` *"how far apart are these on the drawing"*; `DIMENSION_MEP_RUNS` *"how far apart are these pipes on the drawing"*; and `REPLACE_MATERIAL` *"why will this material not purge"*. **TWO ARE WORTH A SECOND LOOK RATHER THAN A REPAIR**, which is why this reports and does not judge: the DIMENSION pair say *on the drawing*, which is arguably an instruction to annotate; and `REPLACE_MATERIAL`'s is a CONSIDERED DECISION with its reasoning in a comment above it - it was moved there in 2026-09-06 because it had been resolving to `FIND_UNUSED_MATERIALS`, *"which lists materials with NO user, and therefore cannot say what is still holding on to THIS one"*. That comment is right about the routing and the sentence is still a question answered by a write; **the repair it actually names is a READ that says what still uses a material**, which nothing provides | **OPEN - TEN FINDINGS, THE SWEEP THAT FINDS THEM IS BUILT, AND NOTHING IN `brain/fragments/` WAS EDITED. Found 2026-09-19.** The state says OPEN on purpose and `tools/open-defects.py` counts a row by its first word: the ten sentences are still declared on writes today, and a cell opening *the sweep is built* would drop this out of the sweep while the unrepaired half sat there - which is [row 118](#)'s shape and [row 153](#)'s. **WHAT IS BUILT:** `tools/check-declared-questions.py` reads the FILES through `heron_fragment.load_all()` - **never the store**, so it holds in CI where there is none and two runs disagree only if somebody edited a fragment ([rows 116](#) and [136](#)). The write line is read from `HeronOperationRegistry.cs` through `generate-jobs.write_threshold()` - Golden Rule 19, imported and not copied, because that function REFUSES to guess when the registry is rearranged - and a risk `HeronRisk` does not name is **reported rather than assumed safe** (D-52). It is now in `gates.yml`'s *reports* job beside the other three, so it runs on every pull request instead of when somebody remembers. **AN IMPERATIVE IS NOT A QUESTION, AND THE PATTERN WAS CORRECTED BEFORE IT LANDED**: the first version included `do` and `did` as auxiliaries and filed *"do the grayout"* as a defect on the fragment that correctly owns it. Both cases are pinned in `tests/test_declared_questions.py`, and a sentence that asks and **then says what to do** - *"which elbow this type inserts, change it"* - is listed separately and never counted, which is the separation `check-risk-crossings.py` already makes for imperatives. **EVERY SENTENCE IN THAT SUITE IS A REAL DECLARED UTTERANCE**, not an invented one: an invented sentence proves only that the pattern matches itself. **IT NEVER SUGGESTS DELETING AN UTTERANCE TO TIDY THE REPORT** - [row 113](#)'s forbidden move is weakening a declaration to buy a number, and deleting a sentence a modeller really says so a sweep comes back clean is the mirror of it. **NOTHING IN `brain/fragments/` WAS TOUCHED**: the ten are a finding for whoever owns those files, and the repair for each is one line - declare the sentence on the READ that should own it - **except where no READ exists**, which is [row 146](#)'s capability gap and a larger thing. **AND ROW 146's ENUMERATION IS RIGHT WHILE THE CONCLUSION DRAWN FROM IT IS TOO STRONG** - measured the same way, and the distinction matters because the repair is different. That row lists the capabilities with SIZE, WORKSET, INSULATION or SCALE **in their names** and says truly that not one of them reports the value. [HANDOVER](HANDOVER.md) then carries it as *"four have no READ to give the sentence to at all"*, and **that does not follow from an enumeration by name**: `READ_ELEMENT_PARAMETERS` (FRG-PAR-001, READ, PROVEN) reads *one named parameter off every element* and **already declares the utterance `get the size parameter`** - a READ that claims size-reading in its own words and has SIZE nowhere in its name. **WHAT IS MEASURED AND WHAT IS NOT:** all four sentences still land on a MODIFY today, asked through `heron_brain.lookup` - `AUTO_SIZE_MEP`, `SET_VIEW_WORKSET_VISIBILITY`, `SET_MEP_INSULATION` and `SET_VIEW_SCALE`. Whether `READ_ELEMENT_PARAMETERS` can actually answer each of them is a **Revit question this container cannot settle** - insulation is a separate element wrapping its host, which `SELECT_BY_INSULATION`'s own purpose warns about, so reading the duct's parameters would not give the thickness. **View scale is the one that is certainly a capability gap**: `SET_VIEW_SCALE` is the only capability in the library with SCALE in its name, and it writes. **AND TWO NEIGHBOURING SWEEPS WERE RUN AND BOTH CAME BACK CLEAN, WHICH BOUNDS THIS ROW RATHER THAN WIDENING IT.** (1) A capability NAMED like a read - `CHECK_`, `REPORT_`, `LIST_`, `FIND_`, `READ_`, `MEASURE_`, `DESCRIBE_`, `AUDIT_`, `COMPARE_`, `COUNT_`, `DIAGNOSE_`, `PROBE_` - declared at a risk that WRITES: **zero of 395**. (2) A capability named like a write - `SET_`, `CREATE_`, `DELETE_`, `MOVE_`, `PLACE_`, `REPLACE_`, `ASSIGN_` and seventeen more - declared at READ or ANALYZE: **one**, `EXTRACT_DATES_FROM_TEXT`, and it is **correct** - it pulls dates out of text notes, extracting information rather than elements. **So the library's NAMES and RISKS agree everywhere**, and these ten are not a pattern of careless declaration: they are ten specific sentences, each with an owner and each repairable in one line. Neither sweep is kept as a tool - a check that has never found anything is a check nobody reads, and the result is recorded here instead. **AND THE SAME RULE, ONE LAYER UP, FINDS FIVE MORE - WHICH IS EXACTLY WHERE [ROW 137](#) SAID THE BLIND SPOT WAS.** A skill declares a risk and a list of utterances just as a fragment does, so the sweep reads both through a four-line adapter rather than a second copy of the rule. Five skills above the write line claim a question: `space-airflow` *"how much air does this room need"*; `sprinkler-layout` *"how many sprinklers do I need"* and *"how many sprinklers for this room"*; `terminal-layout` *"how many diffusers for this room"* and *"how many at 3 m coverage and where"*. **ROW 137 BELIEVED TWO OF THESE WERE ALREADY REPAIRED.** It records that the owner found *"how many sprinklers"* in `sprinkler-layout` by hand and that *"that one was repaired by giving the sentence to `count-elements`"* - and `count-elements` does now declare *"how many sprinklers on level 2"*, a THIRD phrasing, while the two above stayed where they were. **The repair moved one phrasing and left two, and nothing could see it**, for the reason row 137 gives: a crossing compares a sentence's reach against the SKILL's own declared risk, so a question inside a MODIFY skill never registers. ## 5b. HERON'S OWN DEFECTS found by reading the repository, file by file

> Section 5 holds what **proving against a model** found. This holds what **reading** found — the word-by-word sweep of every tracked file that is not `brain/**.yaml`, recorded in [`REVIEW-LEDGER.tsv`](REVIEW-LEDGER.tsv). **How many that is, and how many have been read, is derived and not typed here.**
>
> **They are kept apart because they are found differently and they go stale differently.** A proving defect is re-tested by running the fragment again. A reading defect is re-tested by reading the file again — and the ledger knows when that is necessary, because the file's content hash stopped matching the one recorded when it was read.
>
> **No number is typed here.** Derive it:
>
> ```bash
> python tools/open-defects.py          # both sections, by id
> python tools/review-ledger.py         # how much of the repository has been read
> ```

**THE SWEEP RECORDS, IT DOES NOT REPAIR.** A file found wrong is marked `issue` in the ledger and written up here; the file itself is left alone. A sweep that also fixes things stops being a sweep — the reader loses track of which files have actually been read, and a repair made in passing is a change nobody reviewed. Anything big enough to need its own sitting gets **`OPEN — needs a sitting`** and waits there.

**A ROW HERE IS WRITTEN FOR A SESSION THAT WAS NOT PRESENT.** Name the file and the line; say what is wrong in the words you would use out loud; and say what you did **not** check, so the next reader knows where your reading stopped rather than assuming it was complete.

| # | Defect | State |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **`HeronPaths.IsSafeToDelete` COMPARES PATHS AS TEXT, SO A FOLDER MERELY *NAMED* LIKE HERON'S READS AS SAFE TO DELETE.** [`platform/Heron.Core/HeronPaths.cs:97-106`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronPaths.cs). The check is `full.StartsWith(derived, OrdinalIgnoreCase)`, and `Path.GetFullPath` returns no trailing separator — so with `Derived` = `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Heron`, the path `%LOCALAPPDATA%\HeronBackup\anything` starts with it and the function returns **true**. `Heron-old`, `Heron.bak` and `Heron backup` all do the same. The DATA leg on line 104 has the same flaw but fails **safe** — a stray sibling gets protected rather than deleted — so only the DERIVED leg on line 105 can destroy anything. **IT IS NOT REACHABLE TODAY, AND THAT WAS CHECKED RATHER THAN ASSUMED.** The only caller in the repository is `PruneLogs` at [`revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/HeronApplication.cs:346`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/HeronApplication.cs), which iterates `Directory.GetFiles(LogDirectory, "addin-*.log")`; `LogDirectory` is `private set` and is assigned in exactly one place, line 101, from `HeronPaths.Logs`. So every path that reaches the guard today is genuinely inside DERIVED and the guard's answer happens to be right. **THE DEFECT IS THAT THE NEXT CALLER WILL NOT KNOW.** This is a `public static` function whose whole job is to be the protection, and [`platform/README.md:40`](../platform/README.md) presents it as exactly that — *"`IsSafeToDelete` returns false for anything under data — so an updater cannot touch the user's own"*. Someone adding a cleanup, an uninstaller or a cache wipe will reach for it, pass a path they did not derive from `HeronPaths`, and get a wrong answer with no warning. **NOTHING TESTS ITS BEHAVIOUR.** `tests/test_paths.py` tests the *Python* `PATHS` classifier and greps this C# file as **text** (that the metadata header is present, that certain phrases appear); it never calls `IsSafeToDelete` with a path. So the sibling-folder case has never been run. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether any Heron installer, updater or backup script outside this repository calls it — the grep covered `.cs`, `.py`, `.ps1` and `.md` in this tree only. **THE REPAIR IS ONE CHARACTER PLUS A TEST**: compare against `derived + Path.DirectorySeparatorChar` (and allow the root itself, which docs/06 says is always safe to delete), then give `tests/test_paths.py` the `HeronBackup` case so it cannot come back | **OPEN — recorded, not fixed.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20, as the first file of the repository sweep. Not reachable today, so it is not urgent; it is a loaded gun with the safety on, and the sweep's rule is to write it down rather than to reach into a file it was only reading |

| **2** | **THE STOP REFUSAL TELLS THE USER TO PRESS A BUTTON THAT WAS REMOVED, AND SENDS THEM AWAY FROM THE ONE THAT WORKS.** [`platform/Heron.Core/HeronStop.cs:76-79`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronStop.cs) — *"Heron is stopped. Nothing was sent to Revit. **Press Heron AI > Emergency Stop again** to let it work…"*. That ribbon button was removed on the owner's instruction on 2026-09-06, [D-46](DECISIONS.md#d-46--the-emergency-stop-button-is-removed-the-switch-behind-it-stays), and [`HeronApplication.cs:305-319`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/HeronApplication.cs) says so in a comment where the `AddItem` used to be. **D-46 ALSO NAMES THE REPLACEMENT, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES THIS WORSE THAN A STALE STRING**: *"the Heron button IS that control. It calls `bridge.Stop()`, so nothing can reach Revit at all."* So there is a working control, the message knows nothing about it, and a user reading this refusal would hunt the ribbon for a button that is not there while the one that would help sits in front of them. **IT IS UNREACHABLE TODAY, AND THAT WAS CHECKED RATHER THAN ASSUMED.** `HeronStop.Message` is read in two places — [`RevitOperations.cs:273`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitOperations.cs) and [`RevitWrite.cs:706`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) — both guarded by `IsStopped`. The only thing that calls `Stop()` is `EmergencyStopCommand` at [`Commands.cs:233`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/Commands.cs), which is still a working `IExternalCommand` but has no ribbon entry, so nothing a user can press sets the flag. `HeronApplication.cs:316` states this plainly: *"Nothing can SET the stop any more, so today it is a gate that will never close."* **THE DEFECT IS THAT THE COMMENT ALSO SAYS WHEN IT WILL FIRE.** The switch is kept *"for the file-based kill switch docs/21 section 4 describes and which was never built"* — and on the day somebody builds that, the gate closes, this message is shown for the first time, and it is wrong in the one moment a user most needs it to be right. A refusal is read by somebody who is already worried. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether any Python-side refusal carries its own copy of this sentence — the grep covered `.cs` only. **THE REPAIR IS THE SENTENCE, NOT THE CODE**: name the Heron button as D-46 does, keep the Ctrl+Z line, which is the most useful thing in the message and is still true | **OPEN — recorded, not fixed.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. Unreachable today, so nothing is failing now; it is a message that is correct only because nobody can make it appear. Wording is the owner's call and D-46 is his decision, so the replacement sentence should be his |

| **3** | **`NaN` REACHES A PARAMETER WRITE AND A GEOMETRY POINT, BECAUSE TWO SITES HAND-ROLLED THE RANGE CHECK INSTEAD OF CALLING THE GUARD THAT EXISTS.** [`revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitFragment.cs:1928`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitFragment.cs) (the point parser) and [`:2578`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitFragment.cs) (a `length` parameter value) both parse with `double.TryParse(…, NumberStyles.Float, InvariantCulture, …)` and then test `millimetres > HeronUnits.MaxMillimetres \|\| millimetres < -HeronUnits.MaxMillimetres`. **Both comparisons are FALSE for `NaN`**, so the check passes it, and the next line is `HeronUnits.MillimetresToFeet(millimetres)` — into `new DoubleParameterValue(…)` at 2588, and into `new XYZ(…)` at 1941. **MEASURED, NOT REASONED — AND THE FIRST MEASUREMENT NAMED NO RUNTIME, WHICH WAS A MISTAKE CORRECTED WHEN THIS WAS FIXED.** A console program compiled against **net8.0** ran the exact parse and the exact comparison: `"NaN"`, `"nan"` and `"NAN"` **all parse true** and **all pass the inline guard**; `"Infinity"` and `"-Infinity"` are correctly rejected by it, and `"200000000"` is rejected as intended. `MillimetresToFeet(NaN)` returns `NaN`. **Re-measured on .NET Framework 4.8 — which is what Revit 2020 to 2024 actually run** (`Directory.Build.props`: net472 for 2020, net48 for 2021-2024, net8.0-windows for 2025-2026, net10.0-windows for 2027) — **`"nan"` and `"NAN"` parse FALSE there**, because only .NET Core made the culture's `NaNSymbol` match case-insensitively. **The exact spelling `"NaN"` parses on BOTH**, so the defect was real on every release Heron supports; the lowercase spellings were only ever a way in on **2025 and later**. D-05 does not extrapolate a runtime table, and the original sentence here did. So the hand-rolled guard catches every case it was written for **except** the one that has no ordering. **THE GUARD THAT CATCHES IT IS TEN LINES AWAY AND IS NOT CALLED.** [`HeronUnits.IsUsableMillimetres`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronUnits.cs) rejects all three spellings — the same program confirms it — and its own docstring states this exact hazard: *"A NaN reaching ElementTransformUtils is a geometry operation with no defined result, inside a transaction, on the user's model."* **Of six `MillimetresToFeet` call sites in the repository, exactly one calls the guard** ([`RevitWrite.cs:742`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs)); one converts a literal `1.0`; and the remaining four rely on upstream checks, two of which are these. **REACHABILITY IS THE PART THAT MATTERS.** These parse text supplied as a fragment argument — a parameter value or a point typed or composed upstream. `length NaN` is not an exotic input for a value that arrived from a division; nothing between the text and the transaction re-checks it. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether Revit itself refuses a `NaN` `DoubleParameterValue` or an `XYZ` with a `NaN` ordinate — that needs a real model and is the one question this row cannot answer from source, so **the damage is unestablished while the path to it is proven**. `RevitFragment.cs` has NOT been read in full (4,256 lines); this was reached by tracing every `MillimetresToFeet` call, so **there may be more of these** and the file is still marked unread in the ledger. **THE REPAIR IS TO DELETE BOTH HAND-ROLLED CHECKS AND CALL `IsUsableMillimetres`**, keeping each site's own wording for the refusal — the message is good, the test underneath it is not | **FIXED 2026-09-20, on the owner's instruction, and the fix is to ASK THE PART THAT OWNS THE ANSWER.** Both sites now refuse `NaN` and both infinities with their own sentence — a modeller is told *"is not a real number… a value like that usually arrives from a calculation that did not work out"*, not a bound it did not breach — and the range test underneath is `!HeronUnits.IsUsableMillimetres(...)` rather than a second hand-written pair of comparisons. That is the point of the repair: the hole existed because the bound was written **twice**, once in the part that owns it and once beside the call, and only one copy knew about `NaN`. **Compiled on all eight releases, 2020 through 2027, all seven projects, `tools/check-compile.py` exit 0.** **NOT YET RUN IN REVIT** — compiling is the API surface agreeing and nothing more, and it costs a Revit restart to try. What would settle it is one `length NaN` against a real model, refused with the new sentence |

| **4** | **SMALL, AND WORDING ONLY: the write refusal points at "the lock button", and the ribbon button is labelled "Changes".** [`platform/Heron.Core/HeronPermissions.cs:126`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronPermissions.cs) — *"To turn it on, use the **lock button** on the Heron AI ribbon"*. The button is created at [`HeronApplication.cs:286`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/HeronApplication.cs) with the text **`"Changes"`**; nothing on the ribbon is called *lock*. **THE PADLOCK LARGELY RESCUES IT** — the button's own long description is *"Closed padlock: Heron can read the model and nothing else. Open padlock: Heron may change it"*, so a modeller reading *"the lock button"* and seeing a padlock will click the right thing. **This is recorded because the register's standard is that a refusal names its control exactly** (the same standard row 2 fails harder), not because anyone is likely to be lost by it. Unlike row 2 this message is **live** — it is what a user sees whenever a write is refused. **The repair is one word**, and which word is the owner's | **PARTLY FIXED 2026-09-21 — the RIBBON PATH was wrong too, and that half is not a judgement.** [#213](https://github.com/Ajmalpshaik/Heron-AI/pull/213) renamed the tab to **`Heron`** and the panel to **`AI Bridge`**, and corrected the messages on the Python side — `heron_session.py`, `heron_mcp_server.py` and the bridge client all now say *"Heron > AI Bridge > Heron"*. **It left the two in `platform/Heron.Core` behind**, so this sentence was sending a modeller to a *"Heron AI ribbon"* that no longer exists — and unlike [row 2](#), this message is **live**: it is what they read every time a write is refused. The path now reads *"the padlock button under Heron > AI Bridge"*. **STILL OPEN**: whether the sentence should name the button by its label (`Changes`) or keep describing the padlock is wording, and the owner's. The other missed site is [`HeronStop.cs:77`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronStop.cs), deliberately **not** patched — it is unreachable and [row 2](#) needs the whole sentence rewritten, not its tab name corrected |

| **5** | **THE AUDIT TRAIL CAN SILENTLY LOSE ENTRIES WHEN TWO REVITS ARE OPEN — IN THE ONE FILE WHOSE JOB IS EVIDENCE.** [`platform/Heron.Core/HeronAudit.cs:108-116`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronAudit.cs). `Record` serialises with `lock (WriteLock)` — a **per-process** lock — then calls `File.AppendAllText`, and catches `IOException` so that *"an audit failure must not take down the operation being audited"*. Every Revit session runs its own add-in in its own process, and `CurrentFile()` is `audit-YYYYMM.jsonl` — **one file, shared by both, with a lock that only one of them can see.** **MEASURED, NOT REASONED.** A net8.0 program held the file exactly as `File.AppendAllText` does (`FileMode.Append`, `FileAccess.Write`, **`FileShare.Read`**) and then appended again the way a second process would: `IOException` — *"the process cannot access the file … because it is being used by another process"* — and the file ended with **one line where two were written.** `Record` catches that exact exception and returns, so the caller is told nothing and the trail simply has a hole. **TWO REVITS IS NOT A HYPOTHETICAL HERE.** [Row 95](#) of this register is a defect found *"with two Revits open — `PIPE` and a second model"*, and [`NEEDS-CHECKING.md`](NEEDS-CHECKING.md) already carries *"two Revits not interfering"* as unproven. This row sharpens that item rather than adding a new worry. **THE PYTHON SIDE GOT THIS RIGHT, WHICH IS WHY IT IS WORTH SAYING.** [`brain/heron_audit.py:39`](../brain/heron_audit.py) writes `audit-brain-YYYYMM.jsonl` deliberately — *"matches the glob, so nothing downstream changes"* — so C# and Python never contend. The collision is add-in against add-in only. **THE SWALLOW IS NOT THE DEFECT; THE SILENCE IS.** Not throwing is right — a full disk must not kill a Revit session. But Golden Rule 14 makes the trail evidence, this file is documented as **never pruned** because of that, and a lost line leaves no mark anywhere. The verbose add-in log exists, rotates, and is disposable: a dropped audit entry belongs in it. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** how often two real sessions actually collide — the window is one append, and nothing here measures the odds; only that a collision loses the entry and says nothing. Whether the `.jsonl` reader would notice a gap was not examined. **TWO REPAIRS, EITHER ALONE HELPS**: write with `FileShare.ReadWrite` and a retry, or a named system-wide mutex instead of a `lock`; and in every catch, note the loss in the verbose log so a hole in the evidence is itself evidence | **OPEN — needs a sitting.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. Reachable in the configuration this project actually uses, but it loses a record rather than changing a model, so it is below row 3. Which repair to take is a design call |

| **6** | **TWO CLASSES DEFINE THE WORKFLOW ID, AND THE ONE THE DOCUMENTATION DESCRIBES IS THE ONE NOBODY CALLS.** [`HeronIdentity.NewWorkflowId()`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronIdentity.cs) returns `wf-20260827-192001-0003-a1b2` — time-prefixed, sortable, *"skimmable by eye"*, and its class docstring claims it owns identity under Golden Rule 10, *"the correlation key that ties one sentence to every agent, retrieval, model call and element touched (docs/21 section 13)"*. [`HeronAudit.NewWorkflowId()`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronAudit.cs) returns a bare 12-character GUID slice with no time in it. **A grep of every `.cs` in the repository finds `HeronIdentity.NewWorkflowId` called ZERO times**; the two live callers — [`RevitDispatcher.cs:187`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitDispatcher.cs) and [`RevitWrite.cs:310`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) — both use `HeronAudit`'s. **SO THE AUDIT TRAIL IS NOT KEYED THE WAY IT IS DOCUMENTED.** Nothing is broken by it — the ids are unique enough (48 bits; a collision needs millions of requests) and correlation still works. What is lost is the property the format was designed for: `audit-YYYYMM.jsonl` cannot be sorted or skimmed by workflow id, which is the one thing a time prefix buys. **THIS IS THE EXACT HAZARD A SIBLING FILE WARNS ABOUT IN ITS OWN WORDS.** [`HeronPermissions.cs:150-154`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronPermissions.cs) keeps `SetWriteEnabled` in one place so that a caller writing the key itself is not *"a second definition of this setting, and the day the two disagree the button reports a state the permission gate does not honour."* Identity has that second definition already. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether the Python side mints workflow ids in a third format — the grep covered `.cs` only. **THE REPAIR IS A DELETION, NOT AN ADDITION**: pick one owner. If the time prefix is wanted, point the two callers at `HeronIdentity` and delete `HeronAudit`'s; if it is not, delete `HeronIdentity`'s and correct its class docstring, which currently describes a key nothing produces | **OPEN — recorded, not fixed.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. Nothing fails today; it is drift between code and its own documentation, and which of the two to delete is a design call |

| **7** | **FOUR OF THE TEN KERNEL CLASSES ARE NAMED BY NO TEST, AND THE LEASE'S ONLY COVER IS THE SUITE [ROW 162](#) SAYS THE SWEEP SILENCES.** Counted across `tests/*.py`, which is how this repository tests C# — the suites name the class and read its source: `HeronPaths` 3, `HeronPermissions` 4, `HeronOperationRegistry` 3, `HeronAudit` 2, `HeronConfig` 1, `HeronLease` 1, and **`HeronStop` 0, `HeronUnits` 0, `HeronIdentity` 0, `HeronAtomicWrite` 0.** **`HeronUnits` AT ZERO IS THE ONE THAT MATTERS**, because [row 3](#) above is a units-guard defect: `IsUsableMillimetres` is the guard two callers bypass, and nothing anywhere calls it with a `NaN` to see what it does. The proof for row 3 had to be written from scratch in a scratch console project — that is the gap, stated as a fact rather than as a worry. `HeronAtomicWrite` at zero is the class written **because** two callers lost user data, and `HeronStop` at zero is the emergency stop. **AND THE LEASE'S ONE SUITE IS THE SILENCED ONE.** `HeronLease` is named only by [`tests/test_bridge_roundtrip.py`](../tests/test_bridge_roundtrip.py), which covers it properly — *"a FREE LEASE, OBSERVED WHILE IT IS STILL FREE"*, the claim, the refusal and the renewal. That is the suite [row 162](#) records as skipped by name in `check-gaps.py` because it cannot exit 3, and it is the one suite failing in this branch's sweep for want of a built test host. **So the protection against two chats fighting over one Revit mid-write — the thing `HeronLease`'s own docstring says becomes a hazard "exactly when Heron can change a model, which is now" — is verified by a suite that the repository's own sweep never runs and never names.** Row 162 records the silence; this records what the silence costs. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether a suite exercises any of the four zero-count classes without naming it — unlikely given how these suites are written, but not ruled out; and no judgement is offered on the C# test hosts, which were not read. **THE REPAIR FOR THE LEASE HALF IS ALREADY WRITTEN DOWN** in row 162 — give the suite an exit-3 path and `check-gaps.py` can stop silencing it | **OPEN — recorded, not fixed.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20, while reading `HeronLease`. A coverage finding, not a fault: nothing here is known to be broken, and that is exactly the claim that has never been tested |

| **8** | **`GetBool` IGNORES ITS OWN FALLBACK ON A MALFORMED VALUE, AND THE ONE KEY THAT DEFAULTS TO *TRUE* IS THE ONE THIS HURTS.** [`platform/Heron.Core/HeronConfig.cs:156-163`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronConfig.cs). `GetBool` returns the fallback only when the raw value is **null or empty**; for anything else it returns `true` for `true`/`1`/`yes` and **`false` for everything else** — including a value it simply does not recognise. **ITS SIBLING DOES THE OPPOSITE, TEN LINES UP.** `GetInt` (148-154) uses `int.TryParse` and returns the **fallback** when the value will not parse. So in one class, a malformed number falls back to the default and a malformed boolean does not. **FOR EVERY KEY BUT ONE THAT ASYMMETRY FAILS SAFE.** `write.enabled` and `bridge.autoConnect` both default to `false`, so garbage resolving to `false` is the direction that protects the model. **`ui.activityBanner` IS READ WITH A `true` FALLBACK** — [`HeronApplication.cs:117`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/HeronApplication.cs), `config.GetBool("ui.activityBanner", true)` — and the defaults table says exactly why: *"On by default, unlike everything else here… the other defaults protect the model by staying off, and this one protects the person by staying on. A Revit frozen with no explanation reads as a crash, and somebody who does not know the banner exists is exactly who needs it."* **SO THE CONFIG FILE IS THE USER'S — ITS OWN HEADER SAYS `Owned by you` — AND A PLAUSIBLE SPELLING TURNS THE BANNER OFF WITHOUT SAYING SO.** `yes` works. `on` does not. `enabled` does not. `True ` with a stray character does not. Each of those silently removes the one thing that tells a modeller Revit is working rather than hung, and the value they typed is still sitting in the file looking like it was accepted. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether the banner has any second switch that would make this moot — `HeronActivityBanner.cs` (1,210 lines) has not been read, and this was reached from the config side only. **THE REPAIR IS TO MAKE `GetBool` MATCH `GetInt`**: recognise the false spellings explicitly (`false`/`0`/`no`), and return the **fallback** for anything else — a value nobody can parse is a value nobody stated | **OPEN — recorded, not fixed.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. Reachable by anyone editing their own config, which the file invites; it costs an explanation rather than a model, so it sits below rows 3 and 5 |

| **9** | **THE `NEVER RUN` BANNER ON THE WRITE PATH IS HALF FALSE, AND A SIBLING FILE ALREADY RECORDS FIXING THE SAME STALE SENTENCE.** [`revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs:21-32`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) opens with a banner in capitals: *"============ NEVER RUN ============ … It COMPILES … and that is the whole of what is known about it. **It has never been loaded into Revit and has never moved anything.**"* **That sentence is false.** [`NEEDS-CHECKING.md:484`](NEEDS-CHECKING.md) records the write path running against a real model: *"`preview_move` returns `"approvalToken": "4093166e70b6"`, `move_elements` accepts it, and the reply was `ok: true, moved: 3, blocked: 0, skipped: 0, warnings: 0` with a single undo entry, **"Heron: move ducts up 200 mm"**"*. It has been loaded into Revit and it has moved three ducts. **THE OTHER HALF OF THE BANNER IS STILL GOOD ADVICE, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES THIS WORTH A ROW.** *"Do not point it at a real project until NEEDS-CHECKING.md group D has passed — D3 in particular"* still stands: [D3](NEEDS-CHECKING.md) — **MEASURE ONE**, *"200 mm. Not 200 feet, not 0.656 of anything… the single most important line in this file"* — is not struck through. A move ran; nobody has measured the duct. **So the warning is a mix of one claim a reader can disprove in a minute and one that still matters, and the first discredits the second.** That is a worse state than being simply wrong. **THE REPOSITORY HAS ALREADY MET THIS EXACT SENTENCE AND FIXED IT ONCE.** [`HeronPermissions.cs:114-122`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronPermissions.cs) carries the note: *"THE REASON HAD GONE STALE IN THE TEXT A USER READS. It said the write path 'has never been run against a real model', which was true when it was written and has not been since."* It was corrected in the user-facing refusal and left standing in the source banner. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether any of `RevitWrite`'s seven declared agents carries a signed proof — `brain/agent-proofs/` holds none of them, but the agent-proof route was not traced further. **THE REPAIR IS THE SENTENCE**: say what has run and what has not, and keep D3's warning, which is the part doing the work | **OPEN — recorded, not fixed.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. Nothing executes wrongly because of it; it misleads whoever next opens the most dangerous file in the repository, which is its own cost |
| **10** | **`FailureSeverity.DocumentCorruption` — REVIT'S HIGHEST SEVERITY — IS NEITHER ROLLED BACK NOR COUNTED, IN THE ONLY CODE THAT CAN CHANGE A MODEL.** [`RevitWrite.cs:899-926`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs), `CollectWarnings.PreprocessFailures`. The first loop returns `ProceedWithRollBack` for `FailureSeverity.Error`; the second counts and deletes `FailureSeverity.Warning`; anything that is neither falls through to **`FailureProcessingResult.Continue`**. **MEASURED AGAINST THE SHIPPED ASSEMBLIES, NOT FROM MEMORY** — the standard [`DocumentKey`'s own docstring](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) sets four hundred lines up. Reading `Autodesk.Revit.DB.FailureSeverity` out of `RevitAPI.dll` by reflection: **`None=0, Warning=1, Error=2, DocumentCorruption=3`** — identical in **Revit 2020 and Revit 2024**. So the case that falls through is the one *above* `Error`. **THE FILE'S OWN REASONING ARGUES AGAINST WHAT IT DOES.** *"Errors are different and are never swallowed: an error means Revit could not do what was asked, and continuing past one produces a model that is subtly wrong rather than obviously unchanged. Those roll the whole thing back."* Document corruption is where continuing is worst, and it is the one severity that continues — **and it is not counted either**, so the reply's `warnings: 0` and the audit's `warnings` field both say nothing happened. **NOTHING IN THE REPOSITORY MENTIONS IT.** A grep for `DocumentCorruption` across every `.cs`, `.py` and `.md` outside the handover archive returns **nothing**, and this is the only `IFailuresPreprocessor` Heron has. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** how readily Revit raises this severity during an element move — likely rare, and nothing here measures it; whether Revit would permit the commit afterwards regardless; and **Revit 2027**, whose `RevitAPI.dll` targets .NET 10 and could not be read by the .NET Framework reflection route used here — so 2027 is stated as **unchecked**, not as agreeing. **THE REPAIR IS ONE COMPARISON**: treat `>= FailureSeverity.Error` as roll-back, rather than `== Error` | **FIXED 2026-09-20, on the owner's instruction. One character: `==` became `>=`.** The comparison is `message.GetSeverity() >= FailureSeverity.Error`, so `DocumentCorruption` now rolls the whole group back, and a severity added **above** these in a future release is caught by arriving rather than by somebody remembering to add a branch for it. The docstring carries the enum values and the two releases they were read from, so the next reader does not have to go and look. **Compiled on all eight releases, 2020 through 2027, exit 0. NOT YET RUN IN REVIT**, and provoking a genuine `DocumentCorruption` to watch it roll back is not something this sweep can arrange |
| **11** | **THE "MODEL MOVED ON" REFUSAL REPORTS THE ONE NUMBER THE SAME FILE SAYS CANNOT TELL THE DIFFERENCE.** [`RevitWrite.cs:292-299`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs). The check is right: `now.SetEquals(preview.Ids)` compares the **id set**, exactly as the `Preview` class demands — *"a count alone cannot tell the difference between 'nothing changed' and 'one duct was deleted while another was drawn'. Both give 247. Only one of them is the model the user looked at."* **The message underneath it then reports counts**: *"it described "+ preview.Ids.Count +" "+ preview.Category +" and there are now "+ now.Count*. **In the exact case the docstring names — one deleted, one drawn — that renders as *"it described 247 ducts and there are now 247. Nothing was changed."*** A modeller reads a refusal that gives two identical numbers as its reason and concludes Heron is broken, at the moment it is in fact protecting them correctly. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether the Python side rewrites this message before the user sees it. **THE REPAIR IS TO SAY WHAT THE SET CHECK ACTUALLY FOUND** — how many are new and how many are gone, both derivable from the two sets already in hand | **OPEN — recorded, not fixed.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. The protection works; only its explanation fails, and only in the case that most needs explaining |
| **12** | **THE AUDIT RECORDS WHICH ELEMENTS WERE *ATTEMPTED*, UNDER A COMMENT PROMISING WHICH ONES *MOVED*.** [`RevitWrite.cs:386-419`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) writes `elements` from `UniqueIds(doc, movable)` — the list assembled *before* the transaction. The comment above it says the ids exist to answer *"WHICH ducts did it move?"*. When `blocked` or `unverified` is non-zero, some of those ids did not move, and the record cannot say which. The same block is scrupulous about this distinction in the **counts** — `moved`, `attempted`, `partly`, `blocked`, `unverified` are five separate fields precisely because *"an audit that says 'moved 5' when 5 did not budge is worse than no audit"* — and then writes one undifferentiated id list. `Verify` already knows the answer per element and discards it, keeping only totals | **OPEN — minor.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. The counts are honest; only the id list is coarser than its own comment claims |
| **13** | **`DocumentKey` MIXES IDENTITY WITH LOCATION, SO A `SAVE AS` DURING A PREVIEW IS REPORTED AS THE MODEL HAVING BEEN CLOSED.** [`RevitWrite.cs:784-794`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) returns `identity + "|" + doc.PathName + "|" + doc.Title`, where `identity` is the Project Information `UniqueId` — which its own docstring says *"survives being saved, renamed and moved"*. Appending the path and the title takes that property back out: after a `Save As` the key changes, the Golden Rule 20 comparison at line 251 fails, `IsStillOpen` compares the same composite key and returns false, and the user is told **"X has been closed since that preview"** about a model that is open in front of them under a new name. The file itself says why that wording was chosen with care — telling someone to click back to a model that is not open *"sends them looking for something that does not exist, at the exact moment they are already unsure what just happened to their work"*. Here it does the reverse and is equally wrong. **The title and path are already carried separately on the `Preview` object** for the wording, so nothing is lost by keying on identity alone. **Narrow** — it needs a `Save As` inside the two-minute preview window — and it **fails safe**: nothing is written | **OPEN — minor.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. Refuses correctly, explains wrongly |

| **14** | **THREE DECLARED OPERATIONS NEVER PASS THE GATE, AND THE GATE'S OWN COMMENT SAYS EVERY OPERATION DOES.** [`RevitOperations.cs:40-56`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitOperations.cs) heads the gate with *"Every operation, one place, BEFORE any routing. This is what makes docs/12 section 71 true."* [`BridgeServer.cs:365-425`](../revit/Heron.Bridge/BridgeServer.cs) answers **`ping`**, **`info`** and **`release`** itself and returns, before the lease claim and before `RequestHandler` — which is the only route to `RevitOperations.Run`, and therefore to `Gate`. **THE THREE ARE CORRECT TODAY AND THE REASON THEY SIT THERE IS GOOD.** All three are declared `Read`, which `Gate` would permit anyway; none touches a model; and the comment explains why they must precede the lease — *"claiming in order to release would renew the very thing being given up"*, and *"asking 'who has this?' must not be the act of claiming it"*. Nothing is wrong now. **THE DEFECT IS THAT THE BYPASS IS AN ESTABLISHED PATTERN AND THE GATE EXISTS FOR EXACTLY THAT.** `Gate`'s own justification is *"for the sake of the operation nobody has written yet… An operation that forgot to check its own permission used to write unchecked and nothing noticed."* There is now a second, unguarded place to add an operation — with a worked precedent of three and a stated rationale (*"BEFORE THE LEASE CLAIM, beside ping and info"*) that a future author can honestly follow. A fourth `if` added there inherits the gate bypass for free, and no test would see it. **`tests/test_tool_registry.py` DOES NOT COVER THIS**, and that is not a fault in it: it proves every routed operation is **declared** and every declared one is **routed** — including the `op == "op"` forms — which is a different claim from *every operation passes the gate*. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether the MCP server imposes its own gate before the wire. **THE REPAIR IS CHEAP EITHER WAY**: call `Gate` in `BridgeServer` before the three `if`s, or add a test asserting that the only ops answered outside `RevitOperations.Run` are a named, `Read`-risk allow-list | **OPEN — recorded, not fixed.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. Nothing is unguarded today; a second door has been left where the whole design is one door |
| **15** | **A `select_by_category` WITH NO CATEGORY RETURNS A .NET EXCEPTION MESSAGE, WHILE THE SENTENCE WRITTEN FOR THAT EXACT CASE SITS TWENTY LINES BELOW IT.** [`RevitOperations.cs:438`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitOperations.cs) does `foreach (var part in category.Split(','))` with no null check. [`Json.ReadString`](../revit/Heron.Bridge/Json.cs) returns **`null`** for an absent key — line 150, read rather than assumed — so a request that omits `category` dereferences null. [`RevitDispatcher.cs:298-305`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitDispatcher.cs) catches it (*"Never let one bad request throw out of here"* — correctly, so Revit is never at risk) and returns `Json.Error("operation_failed", ex.Message)`. **What the modeller is shown is therefore: *"Object reference not set to an instance of an object."*** **THE EMPTY-STRING CASE IS HANDLED BEAUTIFULLY, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES THIS A DEFECT AND NOT A GAP.** `""` splits to one empty part, is skipped by the `name.Length == 0` guard, reaches line 458 and returns *"No category was given. Try 'ducts', or several at once like 'pipes, pipe fittings'."* The author wrote the refusal, placed it two lines past the crash, and never reached it. **THE SHARED HELPER GUARDS IT TOO** — `ResolveCategory` opens with `string.IsNullOrEmpty(category)` — so the check exists twice in this file and neither copy runs first. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether the MCP server always sends the key, which would make this unreachable in normal use; the add-in's own rule is that *"the wire is not a place to extend trust"* ([`RevitWrite.cs:723`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs)). **THE REPAIR IS TO TREAT NULL AS EMPTY** at line 438 | **OPEN — minor.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. Nothing is damaged and Revit is never at risk; a modeller is handed a programmer's sentence by a codebase that does not otherwise do that |
| **16** | **`ResolveCategory`'s DOCUMENTATION IS STACKED ONTO `ProjectKey`, SO ONE METHOD CARRIES TWO SUMMARIES AND THE OTHER CARRIES NONE.** [`RevitOperations.cs:517-551`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitOperations.cs). Two `<summary>` blocks sit back to back above `ProjectKey`: the first describes *"A BIM word to a Revit category, or the refusal to give back"* and argues why the table is shared — *"One table means 'ducts' cannot mean OST_DuctCurves when Heron SELECTS and something else when it MOVES — which is the kind of divergence that is invisible in review and obvious only in the model afterwards."* That is `ResolveCategory`, which is defined at line 553 **with no comment at all**. **The paragraph justifying the one table both the read path and the write path depend on now hangs off an unrelated method**, in a repository where the comment is the design record. Behaviour is unaffected; XML doc generation is evidently off, or the duplicate summary would have been a build warning | **OPEN — minor, documentation.** Found by reading, 2026-09-20. A move-the-comment fix |

| **17** | **`tests/test_skill_catalog.py` FAILS ON THE OWNER'S PC — THE CROSS-DRIVE `os.path.relpath` DEFECT, FOR AT LEAST THE FIFTH TIME, IN A FILE THE REPOSITORY ALREADY HAS THE FIX FOR.** Measured 2026-09-20 on the owner's Windows checkout: **exit 1**, `ValueError: path is on mount 'C:', start on mount 'D:'`, raised at [`tools/generate-skill-catalog.py:199`](../tools/generate-skill-catalog.py) — `meta["why"] = "no %s" % os.path.relpath(folder, ROOT)`. `folder` is a **parameter** (`folder = folder or RECORDINGS`), the suite passes it a `mkdtemp()` path, the repository is on `D:` and Windows temp is on `C:`, and `relpath` **raises** across mounts rather than returning something useless. The line only runs when the folder is missing — it is inside `if not os.path.isdir(folder)` — so it is the *absence* branch, written to explain a missing recording, that crashes. **NOT INTRODUCED BY THIS SWEEP.** `generate-skill-catalog.py` appears in none of this branch's commits; `git log` puts its last three changes at `d47488a`, `1ebd0f1` and `3dd7159`. **THIS EXACT DEFECT IS ALREADY IN THE RECORD TWICE, AND WAS ALREADY CALLED RECURRENT.** [`NEEDS-CHECKING.md` A18](NEEDS-CHECKING.md): *"`brain/heron_instructions.py:127` called `os.path.relpath` unguarded … on Windows that RAISES across drives (the test's `mkdtemp()` is on `C:`, the repo on `D:`)"* — fixed. [K1](NEEDS-CHECKING.md): *"`test_authoring` was the Windows cross-drive `os.path.relpath` defect for the **third** time that day, and a **fourth** unbitten copy was found beside it in `heron_tag.py`."* This is the next one. **THE FIX IS ALREADY WRITTEN AND NAMED.** [`brain/heron_fragment.repo_relative()`](../brain/heron_fragment.py) exists for precisely this, and its docstring says so: *"on Windows os.path.relpath RAISES across drives rather than merely returning something useless."* [`tools/check-licence.py:115-125`](../tools/check-licence.py) carries its own guarded copy with the same warning, and [`tools/balance-of-work.py:50`](../tools/balance-of-work.py) a comment about it.
